I see some people critiquing his teaching methods and the way he makes definitive statements. You have to understand that he’s developing a lesson on the dynamics and structure of power. Some generalizations and assumptions have to be made. This is not a debate, it’s a lesson. A lesson that he developed based on his study and experience. It’s meant to provoke and elicit free thought. It’s not the end all be all. Picking apart everything you don’t agree with is absolutely fine.  But acting like he’s supposed to teach and align with your every thought isn’t practical. Enjoy it, and learn from it. Great class and amazing ideas!! Thank you professor!

>
> ​​@forbeatssake1530 There are honest scientists who can help analyze data, but the problem is not science itself, it’s how data can be selected, weighted, or funded. Scientific results are often influenced by who pays for the research.  
>   
> Take the classic meta-analysis comparing animal fat and vegetable oils from 2012. It included eight studies, but one of them, from 1978, had around 10,000 participants, while the others had far smaller samples. That single study disproportionately influenced the final conclusion. Importantly, it was funded by a vegetable oil company.  
> The result helped promote the idea that refined vegetable oils are as healthy as animal fats, which directly benefited the industry and shaped public dietary guidelines for decades in the entire world.  
> This doesn’t mean science is worthless, but it does mean that even meta-analyses are not immune to bias. Methods, funding, and study selection matter.  
> When reading a scientific paper, the most important questions are not only "what does it say?" But "who wrote it?" And "who funded it?" If there is no direct financial interest tied to a product or industry, the findings are generally more reliable, or at least less compromised... So yeah, science isn't immune to corruption. Nowadays you always have to ask yourself "who is benefiting from this?" in order not to fall in this idea of blind faith on science. People take any poor scientific article and use it to promote their product, and science-believers go on blindly. I'm not saying science is bad, but many people really treat it as a religion and never questions it. Many "scientific facts" were debunked, why you believe the "facts" from today are immune to be debunked?

> **@Tsauro-rex**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgxNx7yfTPNGtSfYJn14AaABAg.AOMuzT6IosFAReCy_Pxp5q)  
>
> ​@AlexBrown-js2gxMost of the time, money creation does not cause inflation. The reasons for this are as follows:  
>   
> 1) The scale of central bank money creation is limited.  
> The money created by central banks typically constitutes no more than 5-10% of the total money supply. Therefore, a government has very little direct control over the total supply. The remaining 90-95% is created primarily by commercial banks through the lending process-whenever someone takes out a loan or uses a credit card. Central bank money is used mainly to pay taxes, purchase government bonds, facilitate interbank transfers, or when transactions are settled with physical cash.  
>   
> 2) Financial absorption in advanced economies.  
> In highly financialised economies, such as those in the US or Europe, newly created money is largely absorbed by the financial system (e.g., asset markets). This mechanism allows the Fed to print a trillion dollars per quarter without generating significant consumer inflation. The inflationary pressure is instead redirected, manifesting as asset price inflation in financial markets and real estate. In contrast, in an economy like China's, this money is more directly absorbed by the real economy, financing tangible assets such as railways, bridges, roads, and ports.  
>   
> 3) "Printed money inflation" only occurs at full economic capacity.  
> Printing money leads to inflation only when an economy is operating at full capacity, with all resources - labour, raw materials, industrial capacity, and services - fully utilised. Under those conditions, additional money creates genuine inflationary pressure. However, such conditions of true full capacity are rarely met in practice.  
>   
> 4) Hyperinflation is often due to a production crisis, not money printing.  
> Countries under severe sanctions (like Venezuela) or devastated by war (like Germany after WWI) experience inflation primarily because their productive capacity collapses. With their economies in ruins, even basic demand creates severe inflationary pressure. While governments then resort to printing money to maintain basic economic function, orthodox economists often mistake this money creation for the cause of inflation. I believe it is a consequence of the underlying production crisis. If money creation alone were the cause of hyperinflation, the solution would be simple: stop printing money. Clearly, reality is far more complex.

>   
> if i were a prisoner in plato's cave, and felt apathy and hopelessness over the absurdity of the shadows (is there more than this?), AND I had s2 thinking, I would blame myself because I cannot "agency" myself into enjoying life within the shadows. but if I have s1 thinking, that there are these indifferent gods who put me in this cave, and i became aware of that there are forces outside my control, it puts me back in control because I can proceed to distinguish what is in and outside of my control.  
>

> The student raised a good point and neither he (nor you) listened. Money can be printed out of nothing but the real economy of goods and services is still subject to scarcity. When you print money you are not creating more food or other goods. > ​@AnonymousKek728 Believe it or not, Marx recognized the same dynamic in his analysis of credit and fictitious capital. Early access to loans and financial claims amplifies power, lets some capitalists consolidate wealth, and leaves later recipients like workers and small businesses facing higher prices. Left unchecked, banks naturally direct credit toward rent-extracting activities such as housing speculation, leveraged buyouts, and tuition debt, avoiding production unless there is clear ROI, since overproduction erodes profits. Credit is fundamentally a political and distributive mechanism, shaping who gains and loses, and determining where economic power concentrates.

>
> Well because it's not quite true. Unless you don't understand the concept of scarcity in economics. Money is not scarce, that is correct. But labor, land/capital, and the resulting goods and services are scarce. Scarcity means that it requires some substantial input to achieve the output, and substantial input is finite. You can't just snap the output into existence like money. It is true that we have abundance, but scarcity (in the strict economics sense) and abundance are not exclusive concepts. If he's trying to say that we live in a "post-scarcity" society, you could maybe make that argument in some informal sense, but it's not literally true if you're using the economics definition. It's kind of a breakdown in communication through his conflation of a formal and an informal definition of scarcity. So the student wasn't really "unable to fathom" it, it's just a bit of intellectual laziness on the professors part to make a point. Or (hopefully not) he just doesn't know the formal definition.  

> **@tongpoo8985**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgyJoTL54kYlUPmEWL54AaABAg.AMBAb2x0VkNASVe-xXmr55)  
>
> ​@nickotine8961 no, it "literally" is scarce. The resource itself is finite, but more importantly the labor and capital required to extract it is finite. Production capacity of society must be allocated to extract it and refine it and transport it. Scarcity and abundance are not mutually exclusive concepts in economics. In the informal/colloquial definition, they are opposites, but scarcity has a specific, objective definition in economics, whereas abundance is a subjective, relative term.

Here’s the timeline of the world’s oldest religions:

- Animism & Shamanism → 50,000 BCE onward, still practiced.
- Sumerian & Egyptian religions → early organized polytheisms.
- Indus Valley → Vedic religion → Hinduism → the oldest living tradition.
- Zoroastrianism → 1200 BCE, survives today in smaller communities.
- Judaism → roots in 1200 BCE, still practiced.
- Buddhism → ~500 BCE, spread across Asia, still strong today.
- Christianity → 1st century CE, grew into the world’s largest religion.
- Islam → 7th century CE, now the 2nd largest religion.

 This shows the shift from prehistoric spirituality → ancient polytheism → monotheism/philosophical religions.

I watched the students react clumsily to Jiang's questions, and it's really profound. 
Especially how hard they held onto the concept of scarcity, they are holding onto it because if they don't, then many of the things they said and did under that idea would be invalid, and they are trying their hardest to still make it work.

I spent many years after schooling to break even some of the conditioning we went through in school.
These kids nearly have no ability to meta-analyse or do higher order thinking (the girl is a little better, sorry guys), their minds are stamped with a very firm control circuit shaped by fear of authority, peer pressure and personal inadequacy.
They are constantly trapped into trying to justify their own world view, so much so that they cannot listen to the lecture in relation to the topic - that of how Power works - and cannot think while suspending their own preconceptions.

The bit about the gods and science, the point isn't the fine detail, but how much those concepts represent how the real world works. 
The tale of the gods are abstract but it hints at where a commoner stands in society and gives them the ability to generate wisdom. 
And idealist scientists aren't bad people, but I think the real thing we need to focus on is what kind of science is supported in our society, and how that relates to the powerful remaining in power.

### **@JungleLearners** | 👍 14 likes

🌿🦜Full Study Guide of this lecture: 
I created a study guide below for this lecture and will do the same for the second class next. While nothing can replace the professor’s lecture, organizing the notes helps me gain clarity. I did not add any of my own ideas—everything comes directly from the lecture. If something seems unclear, keep in mind it’s only the first class, so let’s be patient. Also, remember that the professor emphasized we are here to challenge the ideas presented (1:00:12). As he put it, this class is about how to think, not what to think (3:50). 

==================
COURSE OBJECTIVES: 

1. Understand The World: 
⭐Emmanuel Kant’s Perspective on Understanding the world is the following1:07: 
 ■  Noumena are things as they are, independent of our perception. We cannot directly know the Objective Reality of Noumena 1:33.
 ■  Phenomena are things as they appear in our minds. We use our senses to perceive the things of reality 2:02. Our perception alters the world into structures that we can process e.g. Time, Space, etc. 2:31. 
⭐Imagination: We will call this intermediate step in our mind a form of Imagination 3:22.       
⭐Class Objective: Enhance your Imagination to think for yourself, to understand the secret history of humanity and the theory of power underlying it 6:48.

2. Create Your World
 ■  By understanding the world now, and engaging Imagination, you position yourself to live a future the way you want to live and ⭐create a world that is true to you 7:21.
 ■  And, Collectively, you can contribute to the creation of a system that allows for Eudaemonia, the flourishing of your creative, human intellect 1:10:10.


=====================
COURSE METHODOLOGY:
 1. The Present: ⭐Create an Analytical Model that represents present-day geo-politics 4:55. 
 2. The Future: Apply the Analytical Model to make predictions about the future 5:25. 
 3. The Past: Apply the Analytical Model to the past to reveal the secret history of the world 6:03. 


===========================
🟢MONOTHEISTIC WORLDVIEW
 We will classify the geo-political system of the present as a Monotheistic Worldview where Science is the Authority acting as the dominant religion of the culture 40:56. Arising under the Monotheistic Worldview are three ideas that are transformed into tools of power. 
 1. The Individual
 2. Money
 3. The Nation-State 

1⃣The Individual:
Under Science, the Individual is a collection of memories and synapses. If we manipulate these properly, we can solve all our problems. We can control our well-being and command our fates 39:43. 
 ■  Humanity is understood at the level of Individuals independent of any joint community. 
 ■  ⭐The Individual is solely responsible for all personal outcomes, good or bad. 
 ■  Responsibility is Individual, and there is no direct responsibility by the broader community. 
 ■  Over-emphasis on this perspective makes individuals isolated and vulnerable to power applied against them 42:52.
 ■  Pleasure:
     ■ ⭐Happiness in this worldview is Individual Pleasure. 
     ■  Entertainment, Video games, Money, Friends, Love, Vacations. 
     ■  If you are unhappy, look to the authority of science, doctors. 

 
2⃣Money:
📈Money Creation: Or how Central Banking creates Power 7:38 
 ■  Merchants started banks to store their gold and facilitate trade with other merchants. The banks issued paper receipts for the deposited gold i.e. money that could be circulated independent of the gold 13:43.
 ■  The banks printed additional money, too, and they lent out that extra money to earn additional interest income.  
 ■  Now, with more paper money outstanding than gold deposits, bank runs became a problem 19:34  
 ■  To solve the problem of bank runs, banks organized into Partnership relationships to act as Cartels, allowing them to pool their gold to handle local bank runs. This is Central Banking 22:12. 
📉Money Destruction: Or how wars and financial crises reinforce a perspective of money scarcity. 
 ■  Kings need to borrow money from banks to facilitate war to control more territory. So the banks’ control over the capacity to create and lend that money, became a powerful force over the society. 
 ■  The wars destroy wealth reinforcing the perspective that money is scarce 28:42.
 ■  Central Banking also manages Financial Crises which destroy wealth and reinforce the perspective that money is scarce  27:26 
 ■  The perspective of Money Scarcity creates a powerful motivation for the Individual to work as hard as possible 29:01. 
 ■  Nuance: The real value is the work we do, not money itself. 

 
3⃣The Nation-State:
Before Mandatory-State-Schooling, professional education was facilitated through apprenticeship 52:46. Humans are born with the capacity to learn anything if given the chance 53:40. 
 ■  Component to the public school system is a transfer of authority from parent to teachers who represent the Nation-State 59:05. 
 ■  ⚔The first societies with mandatory public schooling were Sparta, the Aztecs, and Prussia (which developed the system we still use today) 55:48. These three were all war-making societies, dedicated to defeating neighbors. Their schools helped cultivate the society to support war for the Nation-State 57:04.
 ■  The Individual identifies not with the Community but with the Nation-State 1:03:01. 

 
🪄An Alchemical Pattern:
 ■  In the context of chemistry, alchemy was the pursuit to transform lead into gold. 
 ■  In the context of this course, alchemy is a metaphor for any pursuit to transform nothing into something, in particular to transform a concept into power. 
 ■  The aforementioned Implements of Power: The Individual, Money, and The Nation-State meet this classification.
 ■  We call it an Alchemical Pattern whenever a mechanism claims to produce something out of nothing. The pattern alerts us to make a closer investigation of what is actually happening 1:07:53.


=========================== 
🟢POLYTHEISTIC WORLDVIEW: 
Before the present Monotheistic Worldview, there was the Polytheistic Worldview. 

Fate:
 ■  Greek mythology explained that powerful gods affect day-to-day human life 37:08 leaving humans subject to Fate. 47:31
 ■  Fate controlled long term outcomes, so humans can only live for today, seize the day 48:55. 
⭐Eudaemonia: Happiness in this worldview is the state of Eudaemonia. Eudaemonia is living to the best of one’s ability within the confines of Fate. It is the flourishing of human intellect as creative people in the community. 


Collective Community: 
⭐Because humans lack ultimate control due to Fate, it is the Collective Community that affect change 42:52. 
 ■  As such, Humanity is understood at the level of the Community not the lone individual 32:49. 
 ■  Humans identify with each other, because they together comprise a community 42:52. 
 ■  Participation in the Collective Community mattered most in the form of generosity to others, e.g. a shared feast.
 ■  In this worldview, the worst punishment was not death, but exile from the community i.e. the ultimate alienation as an individual.

26:00 - This is absolutely wrong. While money can be infinitely printed, resources are limited, and therefore value is limited. 
Money, for the masses, is a means only to exchange value. If you print infinite money, you simply change the ratio of money to the corresponding value, since value is still finite. 

You're correct about corruption in the sense that local/secretive printing of more money will make immediately downstream folks richer, and keep poor people poor since they'll never see that money. But as a system, we *cannot* have infinite money, otherwise it would be detached from value, which would make money meaningless to the masses, which it cannot be for them to continue bartering.

Making more of it just makes the number of it you need to buy things go up. Ex. I need to buy my family food which is X dollars a week. I am not a farmer so I give my time to building railroads for X dollars a week. I now have X dollars to buy food from someone who put their time into growing the food. If we were both given 1 million dollars then he wouldn’t grow the food and I wouldn’t build the railroads? No of course not then society would collapse. What happens is that now our time is just worth more number of dollars. The farmer sells food for X+1 and I sell my time to building railroads for X+1. The professor has a fundamental misunderstanding of financial markets here I think

### **@user-v9t3v** | 👍 12 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgwTKLsl-Q-tonYQIcF4AaABAg)

I have mixed feelings about this lecture. On the one hand, the professor is engaging and knows how to keep an audience hooked by connecting wildly different ideas (money, schools, monotheism, nation-states) into a single grand story about power. That makes for good theater, but as an argument it often feels more like intellectual sleight of hand than careful reasoning.

Take the section on money. Yes, banks create money through lending, but the “$5 million becomes $9.5 million” example is misleading at best. He glosses over balance sheets, regulation, and the fact that money creation isn’t limitless without consequences like inflation or collapse. It’s a simplification dressed up as a revelation.

The same problem shows up with the “individual as a modern invention.” Sure, modernity emphasized individualism, but to claim the concept didn’t exist in earlier societies ignores everything from Greek philosophy to medieval notions of honor. Likewise, blaming monotheism for the rise of money, individuality, and nation-states is such a massive historical leap it borders on myth-making. Money existed in polytheistic societies long before Abrahamic religions, and nation-states are a modern, largely secular construct, not of monotheism.

Schools as indoctrination machines? There’s truth there, but again he paints it as if education has only ever served power. That ignores both the liberating role schools have played (spreading literacy, science, critical thought) and the way many educators actively resist indoctrination.

So much of this lecture is built on flipping viewers' assumptions: money isn’t scarce, schools don’t educate, nations aren’t real, science is religion. These inversions make for a striking narrative, but they also oversimplify complex realities. What’s presented as hidden truth is often just selective framing.

In the end, the lecture is less about explaining history or economics accurately, and more about seducing the listener into a worldview where everything is illusion and power is the only reality. That’s compelling as performance, but if you treat it as analysis, it collapses under scrutiny.

These ideas are fascinating and I really dig this channel, but as an engineer, when he shows those 2 major understandings of the word, to not include what I'd call "hard science" seems disingenuous.  He showed polytheism and what I'd call social science, hard science I'd argue is more if not the most important factor in the modern world.  It teaches truth (or genuinely attempts to), and can empower individuals and groups.  What do I mean with hard science?  Physics, chemistry, biology can teach you how to make machines that work, shows you how electricity works, the motion of the stars and planets so you can accurately track time, teaches you about how plants grow, teaches you how your body works, why you get sick and how to cure it, how energy flows, it goes on and on.  I'm very miffed by him not mentioning the scientific method.  The power of these subjects and relation to philosophy can not be ignored when talking about these grand ideas.

### **@NyteDayUtube**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=Ugx0DCJ617qM4Uhzjep4AaABAg)

@51:10 — Wow. This lecture made me realize I’m not crazy after all. I started questioning these things at a very young age, but whenever I tried to talk about them, people thought I was crazy or said it was impossible. To me, many of society’s ‘rules’ felt like made-up obstacles that keep people stuck in the wheel.

Traveling changed a lot for me. I met more open-minded people who introduced me to ideas and perspectives I had never heard before. I may not agree with everything, but it’s often easier to relate to them than to friends, coworkers, or even family who are too busy working to stop and reflect.

At one point, I even quit three jobs within two years because I felt the need to step back, observe, and search for answers. I was born Roman Catholic, but I started reading the Quran and exploring different perspectives.

For now, the only reason I’m back in the system is to pay off my $175k nursing school loan.

### **@reasonablemayhem6524**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgwKyxOej1LmWRVyrZR4AaABAg)

Thank you for putting out these lectures for free; I was lucky enough to make it though University, but I can only wish that I'd had a professor like you in my philosophy or sociology courses. 

That said, I have significant disagreements with the opening bit about noumena. The core idea that we, as humans, cannot perceive reality in a non-human way, that much is true, but true by definition; any means by which we can perceive reality is necessarily human because humans are doing the perceiving. The same is true of dogs, and whales, and rocks, and literally every other thing that exists; the act of perception can only occur from the perspective of the thing doing the perceiving. That's the reason you can't have complete knowledge of objective reality; non-human experience excludes human experience, you can't experience both. It's a logical contradiction, a category error.

People often say that this means 'objective reality' is therefore unknowable or that we don't actually interact with it, but this is, at least in my opinion, either technically incorrect or very poor framing. Your senses are responses to physical phenomena, to light and smell and texture; to say these things are somehow divorced from the objective reality seems a stretch, at least to me. There can be more content to the objective thing than you perceive, but there cannot be less. Personal confusion about the thing being perceived, certainly, with illusions and lies and such, but a perception is by definition only true to the degree that it interacts with objective reality. We might only interact with a fraction of said reality, but the impossibility of a complete picture doesn't make the picture we have inaccurate or untrue. Further, I'd like to reiterate that these impossibilities are strictly logical, not physical; anything that exists can be interacted with by anything else that exists. There is no segment of physical reality that is somehow barred from us by virtue of what we are; you could be a person in a dog's body, or a person that had memories of being a dog, or perhaps even a dog that had memories of being a person, but you could not be, at the same time, experiencing the world as a dog and experiencing the world as a person, because those experiences are logically contradictory.


This last bit is somewhat nitpicky (even compared to the rest of this post), but I also take issue with the characterization of time and space as not being a part of objective reality. These phenomena are both emergent from the principle of cause and effect, which is the basis for all patterns, which is the only way in which anything can be said to exist or be real. You can't have distinct concepts or objects without space, and you can't have disparate events without time. Perhaps I lack context for what exactly you mean when you say 'objective reality', but I don't think anything lacking those two phenomena would qualify by any useful definition.

I understand that all these concepts are only incidentally relevant to the majority of the lecture as tools for framing the actual content, and I fully support the critical thinking initiative. These are passable introductions for people who haven't yet encountered such philosophical ideas, and I appreciate the fact that you aren't actually teaching philosophy; I can certainly believe that digging into the philosophical nuance would dilute the point significantly, but in the spirit of seeking true and objective perceptions, I felt the need to air things out a little.

Again, thank you for putting this content out where people can find it; too many people nowadays have forgotten how difficult and tedious critical thinking and observation actually is, and how much they can already see if only they actually paid attention.

---

### **@Diabolic_Fire**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgwgxwOXyvnjYRzEpUB4AaABAg)

I have the genuine question which was asked by students but the teacher seemed to misinterpret it or ignore it completely. The world view he generelizes around 25:00 is based on a following: "The money can be used both as a trading tool and as a measurement of power" (Okay); "The money is infinite" (Okay, the logic that lead to this assumption is fine); "There is poverty" (Yep); "The people get the bulk of their knowledge, including finance, through the system (government and its institutions) and personal experience" (Yep). Then the teacher presents the following solution to contradiction (1-3) based on (4): "The system (the people at the top of it) must have an intention to create an artificial poverty, to create a motivation for people to WORK".

While, maybe true, this worldview (which seems to be presented as a gospel btw) completely ignores the simple fact that "The product of work is GOODS, not money. The GOODS ARE FINITE". If everyone could have as much money as they want, they would try to exchange it for as much GOODS as they want. THERE WON'T BE ENOUGH GOODS. So, naturally, another, main-stream world view is: money's value (and hence it's power representativeness) is self-regulated by the goods available at the time, not the other way around.

THE QUESTION: Why? Why do we ignore an obvious fact of GOODS scarsity BEFORE trying to explain everything and present a model?

# Critical Analysis: What the Lecture Conceals

## The Core Deception

You're absolutely right—this lecture is **descriptive masquerading as diagnostic**. It describes symptoms while hiding the mechanisms, then smuggles in an alternative worldview without examining its own foundations.

## What's Conspicuously Absent

### 1. **Monopoly of Violence**
The lecturer never mentions that states exist because they hold the **monopoly on legitimate violence**:
- Why can banks "create money"? Because the state enforces the legal tender laws with police and courts
- Why must children attend school? Because the state will use force (arrest parents, remove children)
- Why do borders exist? Because armed men will stop you from crossing them
- Why can property exist? Because the state will send armed agents to remove trespassers

**Money isn't valuable because of "illusion"—it's valuable because the state accepts it for taxes and will imprison you if you don't pay.**

### 2. **Material Reality and Scarcity**
The lecturer hand-waves away actual scarcity:

**The banking "trick" isn't actually magic**:
- Banks create *credit* (promises), not resources
- If a bank lends $5M for a restaurant, real materials must exist: land, lumber, equipment, labor
- If banks create infinite money without corresponding real resources, you get hyperinflation (Weimar Germany, Zimbabwe, Venezuela)
- Money is a coordination mechanism for distributing **genuinely scarce resources**

**The poverty "paradox" dissolves**:
- You can't print food, housing, or labor
- The lecturer's dismissal of food waste is sophistry—logistics, distribution, and spoilage are real constraints
- Even with "enough food for everyone," getting it to the right place at the right time requires coordination (which prices help solve)

### 3. **Capital and Production**
Zero discussion of:
- How goods are actually produced
- Why some production methods are more efficient than others
- Capital accumulation and investment
- Productivity and technological advancement
- The actual coordination problems that economic systems solve

### 4. **Enforcement Mechanisms**
The lecturer mentions central banking "cartels" can "establish a new enemy to kill the king" but doesn't explore:
- How exactly does enforcement work?
- What makes some groups able to enforce and others not?
- Why do some institutional arrangements persist and others collapse?
- What constrains the powerful from arbitrary action?

## The Smuggled Faith: What IS Being Sold?

Based on the structure, this appears to be smuggling in a form of **primitivist/anarchist utopianism** or possibly **Marxist-adjacent mystification**, but with a curious twist:

### Elements of the Hidden Ideology:

1. **Noble Savage Romanticism**
   - Ancient Greeks were "superior to us"
   - Pre-modern collective life was authentic
   - Traditional apprenticeship trumps all formal education
   - Polytheism was more "honest" than science

2. **Conspiracy Theory Structure**
   - "Secret history of humanity"
   - Everything you know is a lie
   - Powerful people deliberately create suffering
   - The "real" truth is hidden from you

3. **Anti-Modernism**
   - Money, individualism, and nation-states are purely negative
   - Scientific worldview is oppressive vs. polytheistic worldview was liberating
   - Schools exist only to brainwash, not to transmit knowledge
   - Psychiatry exists to create dependence, not to heal

4. **Gnostic Structure**
   - The material world is illusion/false consciousness
   - Special knowledge will liberate you
   - The masses are deceived; the enlightened see through it
   - Reality is fundamentally about belief/imagination, not material constraints

### The Central Sleight of Hand

The lecturer claims to reveal "how power works" but actually:
- **Mystifies power** by making it seem like pure illusion/imagination
- **Obscures material basis** of social organization
- **Removes agency** from historical actors (it was all "accidental")
- **Eliminates practical alternatives** (if it's all imagination, how do you build different institutions?)

## Why This Matters: The Diagnostic Gap

A genuine diagnostic analysis would address:

### Real Questions About Money:
- Why did commodity money (gold, silver) emerge independently across cultures?
- What coordination problems does money solve?
- Why do some monetary systems collapse and others persist?
- How do you manage resource allocation in complex societies without price signals?
- What's the relationship between credit expansion and real economic growth?

### Real Questions About Power:
- What enables some groups to dominate others? (Military technology, organization, logistics)
- Why do institutions take the forms they do? (What problems are they solving?)
- What are the material constraints on state action?
- How do enforcement costs limit what power can accomplish?
- Why do some revolutions succeed and others fail?

### Real Questions About Modernity:
- Why did the scientific revolution happen in Europe and not elsewhere?
- What are the actual tradeoffs between traditional and modern social organization?
- How did increased productivity change social possibilities?
- What problems does mass education actually solve (even if imperfectly)?

## The Performative Contradiction

The lecturer says: "Question everything, including me!"

But the framework prevents genuine questioning by:
- Dismissing counterarguments as "brainwashing"
- Claiming anyone who disagrees is trapped in "false consciousness"
- Providing no falsifiable claims
- Offering no material alternative (just "reimagine")

When a student correctly notes that resources ARE scarce, the lecturer says: "You guys are stuck in the scarcity mindset... no matter what I say to you, you still believe this."

**This is not intellectual liberation—it's an unfalsifiable belief system.**

## What a Real Diagnosis Would Look Like

### On Banking:
"Banks create credit through fractional reserve lending. This works because: (1) the state enforces contracts, (2) not everyone withdraws simultaneously, (3) central banks act as lenders of last resort. The system enables investment beyond current savings, increasing productivity. The tradeoff is: more economic growth but also boom-bust cycles and inequality. Here are the material constraints on this system..."

### On Education:
"Mass schooling emerged because: (1) industrial economies needed literate workers, (2) nation-states needed standardized populations, (3) parents couldn't teach specialized knowledge, (4) apprenticeship doesn't scale. The tradeoffs are: universal literacy but conformity, broad knowledge but shallow expertise, credential inflation but reduced nepotism..."

### On Nation-States:
"Nation-states emerged because: (1) they could field larger armies through taxation, (2) print capitalism created linguistic standardization, (3) they could coordinate infrastructure at scale. The tradeoffs are: larger peace zones but world wars, individual rights but conscription, rule of law but bureaucracy..."

## The Fundamental Evasion

The lecturer never addresses: **If everything is just imagination and illusion maintained by brainwashing, why can't you just imagine something different?**

The answer they avoid: **Because material reality constrains what's possible.**

- You can't imagine away the need for food production
- You can't imagine away coordination problems in societies of millions
- You can't imagine away the fact that some groups can use violence more effectively than others
- You can't imagine away the tradeoffs between different social organizations

## Conclusion: Description vs. Diagnosis

**Description**: "Banks create money out of nothing through lending"
- This is true (in a limited sense)

**False Diagnosis**: "Therefore money is pure illusion maintained by brainwashing"

**Actual Diagnosis**: "Banks create credit claims on real resources. This works when: (1) state enforcement exists, (2) real productive capacity increases, (3) expectations remain stable. It fails when these conditions aren't met, revealing that money isn't pure illusion but a coordination mechanism with material foundations."

The lecture offers mystification disguised as demystification—exactly the kind of ideological move it claims to oppose.

**What you've identified**: The lecture describes surface phenomena while concealing the deep structure of material constraints, enforcement mechanisms, and institutional tradeoffs. It sells a faith in "reimagination" while avoiding the hard questions about power, production, and the organization of complex societies.

No concept of the individual either like Julius Caesar perfectly understanding his place in history, nor the concept of the nation-state where Rome very much felt it was superior to the nations around it and that being a legal citizen of Rome did not give you special standing amongst citizens of other nations.

Perhaps there are differences in the degree to which we are individualistic today, between modern fiat currency and older forms of money, and modern nation-states compared to ancient ones, but these concepts certainly existed and would not be alien to a person who existed well over a thousand years ago.

It represents a state of mind or a societal structure that traps people and separates them from reality, a "spiritual prison" more than a literal one.

> **@scottridge322**

>
> I think there’s two different situations that you’re kinda combining into one hypothetical. Infinite money is kind of an oxymoron, because it essentially makes its value worthless (unnecessary)—if distributed evenly.   
>   
> On the other hand, just because there is unlimited money… doesn’t mean YOU have unlimited money.   
>   
> In either case, everyone would still be “working” for themselves. (agriculture,trans,smiths,mechanics,doctors, carpenters, etc). Assuming theres an infinite money supply, the currency is now irrelevant.   
>   
> The motivation for labor would be want/need. For example let’s say I need my roof replaced. In our hypothetical, I either have to learn how to do it— or find someone who does (you). And let’s say you tell me, you’d love to fix my roof, but your truck is broke down and needs mechanical repair in order to get the job done. The fastest way to get both done would be to trade services. We both benefit.

From my own experience I know it's a hard to find a specialist who you click with on a human level. But if you do find one with whom it clicks, they are a 'neutral party' with specific knowledge/expertise to confront you with your actions, habits, whatever is disrupting you. It is in fact very confrontational. The job of the specialist is not to comfort you but to give you tools and more objective insight on how to resolve your ingrained thinking/habits,... Realizing and rewiring is not always a happy proces, it's through the hardship and discomfort (experience) that you actually learn. You can even bring in the polytheistic aspect in here (you are too proud or pity yourself too much, you need to get humbled by a different god to balance out)
For sure there are specialists who just do it for the "extraction" culture, using you and your griefs for money. But there are absolutely dedicated specialists who actively try to rewire you with your consent purely for your well being and eventually for the well being of others. I disagree with Professor Jiang on what he said about money. He doesn't mention that the kind of money he's referring to is fiat money, and there are actually different types of money. Also, money is not the same as wealth — money is used to buy wealth.
One point he raised was about why governments don’t just print infinite amounts of fiat money. The truth is, they already have — and that’s what leads to inflation. When more money is printed without a corresponding increase in goods, the value of money drops. Simply put, there’s more money than goods, so the demand for fiat money decreases and its purchasing power declines.

### **@quixotl**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=Ugwk5pGAlaQQBHmDhJh4AaABAg)

The common wisdom explanation of currency developing to facilitate barter is entirely wrong. Currency developed to facilitate state taxation, centralization of surplus, and economic direction of society. Barter was never the common economic form of exchange, Personal credit and debt are ancient and pervasive social forms predating banks by millennia (to say nothing of gift, sharing and mutual aid). Precious metals and stones served as common stores of value for exchange even by merchants across states without the need for minting of currencies. When Jesus said “render unto Caesar that which is Caesar’s,” the state’s role in the invention and perpetuation of currencies is the context he’s referring to.

A brilliant professor, but his understanding of money is a bit flawed. Money is a representation of access to the goods and services that a society produces and has access to. A wealthy society produces more goods and services than a poor society, both in quantity and quality. More prosperous societies have money with greater purchasing power and value than money in poorer societies. If you print more money than the real growth of the economy, you will have more money than goods, causing inflation and devaluing the currency. Governments generally issue debt in their own currencies and practice causing inflation to devalue their own debt, reducing the purchasing power of their own population. Being rich in money or debt assets is not being truly rich. You need to own real assets, which is why socialist governments tend to seize assets, preventing the population from having any real wealth and thus any autonomy.

### **@Andy-bi3uv**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgzV6voQI0yTlLqalO54AaABAg)

The professor seems to repeatedly conflate money and resources as if they're the same thing. They're not. It's fine to claim that there's no scarcity of money because nowadays money is ultimately just numbers in a database, but it's not accurate to also claim that there's no scarcity of resources - this is obviously nonsense as virtually all resources are finite. Money is used to acquire resources, so whether you like it or not, money DOES intrinsically have a degree of finiteness too. While money is, in theory, infinite - creating more just creates inflation - it doesn't actually give you any more purchasing power. So you can create all of the money you like, but it's not going to feed / heat / clothe more people because the price of the finite resources required to do so simply increases in proportion with the money supply. So to claim that money is infinite is meaningless and misleading - it's a logical fallacy.

### **@mono90286**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgzpBQtL17wX-lAeGHF4AaABAg)

Ok this guy is a clown. I came into this with an open mind but the whole "you can't get it out of your heads that money isn't scarce" bit really damaged his credibility. Money is infinite, yes, but resources aren't. Money is not value, it's a representation of value. You can print as many tickets to the theatre as you want, it doesn't make the number of seats in the theatre any larger. You can print as much money as you want, it doesn't make the grain grow any faster. I believe that this was the point being made by the students, although they weren't very articulate stating it. 

When discrepencies emerge between the amount of value in an economy and how that value is represented, the economy shifts to equalise this discrepency. When there are more units of currency going around, but value has not increased, then the way the economy equalises is by each individual unit of currency representing a smaller portion of the value, in other words being worth less. Concretely, this means prices going up, so that while you may have the exact same amount of currency, you can afford less and less stuff. This phenomenon is known as inflation, and is why you cannot just print infinite money. 

Just to be clear, I agree with his point that nowadays resources might as well be unlimited, and that poverty exists because resources are distributed unevenly, in a system maintained by those in power. However, it is vital to include the caveat that this is an incredibly recent phenomenon, emerging due to massive advances in agricultural and industrial technology. Back in the day, before modern science, when the system of central banking he is describing formed, poverty genuinely did exist due to scarcity. The lies of the powerful that resources are scarce in modern society is grounded in this fact. As they say, the most effective lies have an element of truth to them.

### **@BekyCodes-i7p**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lt8XLz78ZvY&lc=UgzMMFPrIZ89P3OiAix4AaABAg)

Here's what GPT has to say to the question Is Reality What We Imagine It to Be? Is It What I Choose?
This is where your questions get to the core of a common misunderstanding. The answer is a firm no, but with a critical nuance.

Kant is not a subjective idealist like George Berkeley, who claimed "to be is to be perceived." Kant is a transcendental idealist who is also an empirical realist.

It is NOT what we "imagine" or "choose": The process of constituting experience is not voluntary or individualistic. You cannot choose to see a world without space, just as you cannot choose to see causes as unrelated events. The "spectacles" of space, time, and the Categories are universal and necessary for all human minds. They are the fixed, a priori "operating system" of human cognition.

The "We" is Transcendental, not Personal: The "we" and "our mind" in Kant's philosophy refers to the transcendental structure of any possible rational mind like ours. It's not about your personal preferences or my individual imagination. This is why we can all agree on the objective truths of science and mathematics—we are all running on the same cognitive hardware.

So, while the world of phenomena is mind-dependent, it is not dependent on your individual, whimsical mind. It is dependent on the universal structures of human cognition, which ensure a single, shared, objective world of experience.
